His brow<br>Is graved and seems to bury what it frowns on,<br>Yet sometime ’tis not so, but alters to<br>The quality of his thoughts. Long [[Time\|time]] his eye<br>Will dwell upon his object. [[Melancholy]]<br>Becomes him nobly—so does [[Arcite]]’s mirth.<br>But [[Palamon]]’s sadness is a kind of mirth,<br>So mingled as if mirth did make him sad<br>And sadness merry. Those darker humours that<br>Stick misbecomingly on others, on them<br>Live in fair dwelling.<br>Cornetts. Trumpets sound as to a charge<br>Hark, how yon spurs to spirit do incite<br>The princes to their proof. [[Arcite]] may win me,<br>And yet may [[Palamon]] wound [[Arcite]] to<br>The spoiling of his figure. O, what pity<br>Enough for such a chance! If I were by<br>I might do hurt, for they would glance their eyes<br>Toward my seat, and in that motion might<br>Omit a ward or forfeit an offence<br>Which craved that very [[Time\|time]]. It is much better<br>Cornetts. A great cry and noise within, crying, ‘A [[Palamon]]’<br>I am not there. O better never born,<br>Than minister to such harm. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Servant |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | What is the chance? |
| SERVANT | The cry’s ‘A [[Palamon]]’. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Then he has won. ’Twas ever likely—<br>He looked all grace and [[success]], and he is<br>Doubtless the prim’st of men. I prithee run<br>And tell me how it goes. |
| *(didascalia)* | Shout and cornetts, crying, |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | ‘A [[Palamon]]’ |
| SERVANT | Still ‘[[Palamon]]’. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Run and enquire. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Servant |
| *(didascalia)* | She speaks to the picture in her right hand |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Poor servant, thou hast lost.<br>Upon my right side still I wore thy picture,<br>[[Palamon]]’s on the left. Why so, I know not.<br>I had no end in’t, else chance would have it so.<br>Another cry and shout within and cornetts<br>On the sinister side the heart lies—[[Palamon]]<br>Had the best-boding chance. This burst of clamour<br>Is sure the end o’th’ combat. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Servant |
| SERVANT | They said that [[Palamon]] had [[Arcite]]’s [[Body\|body]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Within an inch o‘th’ pyramid—that the cry |
| SERVANT | Was general ‘A [[Palamon]]’. But anon<br>Th’assistants made a brave redemption, and<br>The two bold titlers at this instant are<br>Hand to hand at it. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Were they metamorphosed<br>Both into one! O why? There were no woman<br>Worth so composed a man: their single share,<br>Their nobleness peculiar to them, gives<br>The prejudice of disparity, value’s shortness,<br>To any lady breathing—<br>Cornetts. Cry within, ‘[[Arcite]], [[Arcite]]’<br>More exulting?<br>‘[[Palamon]]’ still? |
| SERVANT | Nay, now the sound is ‘[[Arcite]]’. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | I prithee, lay attention to the cry.<br>Cornetts. A great shout and cry, ‘[[Arcite]], victory!’<br>Set both thine ears to th’ business. |
| SERVANT | The cry is<br>‘[[Arcite]]’ and ‘Victory’—hark, ‘[[Arcite]], victory!’<br>The combat’s consummation is proclaimed<br>By the [[Wind\|wind]] instruments. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Half sights saw<br>That [[Arcite]] was no babe. God’s lid, his richness<br>And costliness of spirit looked through him—it could<br>No more be hid in him than [[Fire\|fire]] in flax,<br>Than humble banks can go to law with waters<br>That drift winds force to raging. I did think<br>Good [[Palamon]] would miscarry, yet I knew not<br>Why I did think so. Our reasons are not prophets<br>When oft our fancies are. They are coming off—<br>Alas, poor [[Palamon]].<br>She puts away the pictures.<br>Cornetts. Enter [[Theseus]], [[Hippolyta]], Pirithous,<br><br>[[Arcite]] as victor, and attendants |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Lo, where our sister is in expectation,<br>Yet quaking and unsettled. Fairest Emily,<br>The gods by their divine arbitrament<br>Have given you this knight. He is a good one |
| *(didascalia)* | As ever struck at head. To [[Arcite]] and [[Emilia]] Give me |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | your hands.<br>(To [[Arcite]]) Receive you her, (to [[Emilia]]) you him: (to<br>both) be plighted with<br>A [[Love\|love]] that grows as you [[Decay\|decay]]. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | [[Emilia]],<br>To buy you I have lost what’s dearest to me<br>Save what is bought, and yet I purchase cheaply<br>As I do rate your value. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[Emilia]])<br>O lovèd sister,<br>He speaks now of as brave a knight as e’er<br>Did spur a noble steed. Surely the gods<br>Would have him die a bachelor lest his race<br>Should show i’th’ world too godlike. His behaviour<br>So charmed me that, methought, Alcides was<br>To him a sow of lead. If I could praise<br>Each part of him to th‘all I have spoke, your [[Arcite]]<br>Did not lose by’t; for he that was thus good,<br>Encountered yet his better. I have heard<br>Two emulous Philomels beat the ear o’th’ [[Night\|night]]<br>With their contentious throats, now one the higher,<br>Anon the other, then again the first,<br>And by and by out-breasted, that the sense<br>Could not be judge between ’em—so it fared<br>Good space between these kinsmen, till heavens did<br>Make hardly one the winner. (To [[Arcite]]) Wear the<br>garland<br>With joy that you have won.—For the subdued,<br>Give them our present justice, since I know<br>Their lives but pinch ’em. Let it here be done.<br>The scene’s not for our seeing; go we hence<br>Right joyful, with some sorrow. (To [[Arcite]]) Arm your<br>prize;<br>I know you will not lose her. [[Hippolyta]],<br>I see one eye of yours conceives a tear,<br>The which it will deliver. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Is this winning?<br>O all you heavenly powers, where is your [[Mercy\|mercy]]?<br>But that your wills have said it must be so,<br>And charge me live to comfort this unfriended,<br>This miserable prince, that cuts away<br>A life more worthy from him than all women,<br>I should and would die too. |
| HFPPOLYTA | Infinite pity<br>That four such eyes should be so fixed on one<br>That two must needs be blind for’t. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | So it is. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter, guarded, [[Palamon]] and his three Knights pinioned; enter with them the Jailer and an executioner with block and axe |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | There’s many a man alive that hath outlived<br>The [[Love\|love]] o’th’ people; yea, i’th’ selfsame state<br>Stands many a father with his [[Child\|child]]: some comfort<br>We have by so considering. We expire,<br>And not without men’s pity; to live still,<br>Have their good wishes. We prevent<br>The loathsome misery of age, beguile<br>The gout and rheum that in lag hours attend<br>For grey approachers; we come towards the gods<br>Young and unwappered, not halting under crimes<br>Many and state—that sure shall please the gods<br>Sooner than such, to give us nectar with ’em,<br>For we are more clear spirits. My dear kinsmen,<br>Whose lives for this poor comfort are laid down,<br>You have sold ’em too too cheap. |
| FIRST KNIGHT | What ending could be<br>Of more content? O’er us the victors have<br>Fortune, whose title is as momentary<br>As to us [[Death\|death]] is certain—a grain of [[Honour\|honour]]<br>They not o’erweigh us. |
| SECOND KNIGHT | Let us bid farewell, And with our patience anger tott’ring fortune,<br>Who at her certain’st reels. |
| THIRD KNIGHT | Come, who begins? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | E’en he that led you to this banquet shall<br>Taste to you all. (To the<br>jailer) Aha, my friend, my<br>friend,<br>Your gentle daughter gave me [[Freedom\|freedom]] once;<br>You’ll see’t done now for ever. Pray, how does she?<br>I heard she was not [[Well\|well]]; her kind of ill<br>Gave me some sorrow. |
| JAILER | Sir, she’s [[Well\|well]] restored<br>And to be married shortly. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | By my short life,<br>I am most glad on’t. ’Tis the latest thing<br>I shall be glad of. Prithee, tell her so;<br>Commend me to her, and to piece her portion<br>Tender her this.<br>He gives his purse |
| FIRST KNIGHT | Nay, let’s be offerers all. |
| SECOND KNIGHT | Is it a maid? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Verily, I think so—<br>A right good creature more to me deserving<br>Than I can quit or speak of. |
| ALL THREE KNIGHTS | Commend us to her.<br>They give their purses |
| JAILER | The gods requite you all, and make her thankful. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Adieu, and let my life be now as short<br>As my leave-taking.<br>He lies on the block |
| FIRST KNIGHT | Lead, courageous cousin. |
| SECOND | and THIRD KNIGHTS We’ll follow cheerfully.<br>A great noise within: crying, ‘Run! Save! Hold!’ |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter in haste a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | Hold! Hold! O, hold! Hold! Hold! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Pirithous in haste |
| PIRITHOUS | Hold, ho! It is a cursèd haste you made<br>If you have done so quickly! Noble [[Palamon]],<br>The gods will show their glory in a life<br>That thou art yet to lead. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Can that be,<br>When [[Venus]], I have said, is false? How do things<br>fare? |
| PIRITHOUS | Arise, great sir, and give the tidings ear<br>That are most rarely sweet and bitter. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | What<br>Hath waked us from our dream? |
| PIRITHOUS | List, then: your cousin,<br>Mounted upon a steed that Emily<br>Did first bestow on him, a black one owing<br>Not a hair-worth of white—which some will say<br>Weakens his price and many will not buy<br>His goodness with this note; which superstition<br>Here finds allowance—on this [[Horse\|horse]] is [[Arcite]]<br>Trotting the stones of Athens, which the calkins<br>Did rather tell than trample; for the [[Horse\|horse]]<br>Would make his length a mile, if’t pleased his rider<br>To put [[Pride\|pride]] in him. As he thus went counting<br>The flinty pavement, dancing, as ‘twere, to th’ music<br>His own hooves made—for, as they say, from iron<br>Came music’s origin—what envious flint,<br>Cold as old [[Saturn]] and like him possessed<br>With [[Fire\|fire]] malevolent, darted a spark,<br>Or what fierce sulphur else, to this end made,<br>I comment not—the hot [[Horse\|horse]], hot as [[Fire\|fire]],<br>Took toy at this and fell to what disorder<br>His power could give his will; bounds; comes on end;<br>Forgets school-doing, being therein trained<br>And of kind manège; pig-like he whines<br>At the sharp rowel, which he frets at rather<br>Than any jot obeys; seeks all foul means<br>Of boist’rous and rough jad’ry to disseat<br>His lord, that kept it bravely. When naught served,<br>When neither curb would crack, girth break, nor<br>diff’ring plunges<br>Disroot his rider whence he grew, but that<br>He kept him ‘tween his legs, on his hind hooves—<br>On end he stands—<br>That [[Arcite]]’s legs, being higher than his head,<br>Seemed with strange art to hang. His victor’s wreath<br>Even then fell off his head; and presently<br>Backward the jade comes o’er and his full poise<br>Becomes the rider’s load. Yet is he living;<br>But such a vessel ’tis that floats but for<br>The surge that next approaches. He much desires<br>To have some speech with you—lo, he appears. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Theseus]], [[Hippolyta]], [[Emilia]], and [[Arcite]] in a chair borne by attendants |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | O miserable end of our alliance!<br>The gods are mighty. [[Arcite]], if thy heart,<br>Thy worthy manly heart, be yet unbroken,<br>Give me thy last words. I am [[Palamon]],<br>One that yet loves thee dying. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Take [[Emilia]],<br>And with her all the world’s joy. Reach thy hand—<br>Farewell—I have told my last hour. I was false,<br>Yet never treacherous. Forgive me, cousin—<br>One kiss from fair [[Emilia]]—(they kiss) ’tis done.<br>Take her; I die.<br>He dies |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Thy brave [[Soul\|soul]] seek Elysium. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to [[Arcite]]’s [[Body\|body]])<br>I’ll close thine eyes, Prince. Blessed souls be with thee.<br>Thou art a right good man, and, while I live,<br>This day I give to [[Tears\|tears]]. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | And I to [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | In this place first you fought, e’en very here<br>I sundered you. Acknowledge to the gods<br>Our thanks that you are living.<br>His part is played, and, though it were too short,<br>He did it [[Well\|well]]. Your day is lengthened and<br>The blissful dew of heaven does arrouse you.<br>The powerful [[Venus]] [[Well\|well]] hath graced her altar,<br>And given you your [[Love\|love]]; our master, Mars,<br>Hath vouched his oracle, and to [[Arcite]] gave<br>The grace of the contention. So the deities<br>Have showed due justice.—Bear this hence. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt attendants with [[Arcite]]’s [[Body\|body]] |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | cousin,<br>That we should things [[Desire\|desire]] which do cost us<br>The loss of our [[Desire\|desire]]! That naught could buy<br>Dear [[Love\|love]], but loss of dear [[Love\|love]]! |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Never fortune<br>Did play a subtler game—the conquered triumphs,<br>The victor has the loss. Yet in the passage<br>The gods have been most equal. [[Palamon]],<br>Your kinsman hath confessed the right o’th’ lady<br>Did lie in you, for you first saw her and<br>Even then proclaimed your fancy. He restored her<br>As your stol’n [[Jewel\|jewel]], and desired your spirit<br>To send him hence forgiven. The gods my justice<br>Take from my hand, and they themselves become<br>The executioners. Lead your lady off,<br>And call your [[lovers]] from the stage of [[Death\|death]],<br>Whom I adopt my friends. A day or two<br>Let us look sadly and give grace unto<br>The funeral of [[Arcite]], in whose end<br>The visages of bridegrooms we’ll put on<br>And smile with [[Palamon]], for whom an hour,<br>But one hour since, I was as dearly sorry<br>As glad of [[Arcite]], and am now as glad<br>As for him sorry. O you heavenly charmers,<br>What things you make of us! For what we lack<br>We laugh, for what we have, are sorry; still<br>Are children in some kind. Let us be thankful<br>For that which is, and with you leave dispute<br>That are above our question. Let’s go off<br>And bear us like the [[Time\|time]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Epilogue |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Epilogue |
| EPILOGUE | I would now ask ye how ye like the play,<br>But, as it is with schoolboys, cannot say.<br>I am cruel fearful. Pray yet stay awhile,<br>And let me look upon ye. No man smile?<br>Then it goes hard, I see. He that has<br>Loved a young handsome wench, then, show his<br>face—<br>’Tis strange if none be here—and, if he will,<br>Against his [[Conscience\|conscience]] let him hiss and kill<br>Our market. ’Tis in vain, I see, to stay ye.<br>Have at the worst can come, then! Now, what say ye?<br>And yet mistake me not—I am not bold—<br>We have no such cause. If the tale we have totd—<br>For ’tis no other—any way content ye,<br>For to that honest purpose it was meant ye,<br>We have our end; and ye shall have ere long,<br>I dare say, many a better to prolong<br>Your old loves to us. We and all our might<br>Rest at your service. Gentlemen, good [[Night\|night]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exit |
| FURTHER READING | by SUSAN BROCK |

press<br>Being your slave, what should I do but tend<br>Beshrew that heart that makes my heart to groan<br>Betwixt mine eye and heart a league is took<br>But be contented when that fell arrest<br>But [[Time\|time]], blunt thou the [[Lion\|lion]]’s paws<br><br>Farewell—thou art too dear for my possessing<br>For shame deny that thou bear’st [[Love\|love]] to any<br>From fairest creatures we [[Desire\|desire]] increase<br>From you have I been absent in the [[spring]]<br>Full many a glorious morning have I seen<br><br>How can I then return in happy plight<br>How can my muse want subject to play’st<br>How sweet and lovely dost thou make the shame<br><br>I grant thou wert not married to my muse<br>I never saw that youpainting need<br>If my dear [[Love\|love]] were but the [[Child\|child]] of state<br>If the dull substance of my flesh were thought<br>If there be nothing new, but that which is<br>If thou survive my [[Well\|well]]-contented day<br>If thy [[Soul\|soul]] check thee that I come so near<br>In [[Faith\|faith]], I do not [[Love\|love]] thee with mine eyes<br>In loving thee thou know’st I am forsworn<br>In the old age black was not counted fair<br>Is it for [[Fear\|fear]] to wet a widow’s eye<br>Is it thy will thy image should keep open<br><br>Let me confess that we two must be twain<br>Let me not to the [[Marriage\|marriage]] of true minds<br>Let not my [[Love\|love]] my sin, and thy dear virtue hate<br>[[Love]] is too young to know what [[Conscience\|conscience]] is I<br><br>Mine eye and heart are at a mortal war<br>Mine eye hath played the painter, and hath steeled |
| *(didascalia)* | Music to hear, why hear’st thou music sadly? |
| THE | My glass shall not persuade me I am old<br>My [[Love\|love]] is as a fever, [[Longing\|longing]] still<br>My [[Love\|love]] is strengthened, though more weak in seeming<br>My mistress’ eyes are nothing like the sun<br>My tongue-tied muse in manners holds her still<br><br>No longer mourn for me when I am dead<br>No more be grieved at that which thou hast done<br>No, [[Time\|time]], thou shalt not boast that I do change!<br>Not from the [[Stars\|stars]] do I my judgement pluck<br>Not marble nor the gilded monuments<br>Not mine own fears nor the prophetic [[Soul\|soul]]<br><br>O, call not me to justify the wrong<br>O, for my sake do you with fortune chide win<br>O, from what power hast thou this powerful might<br>O, how I faint when I of you do write<br>O how much more doth [[Beauty\|beauty]] beauteous seem<br>O, how thy worth with manners maysing<br>O, lest the world should task you to recite<br>O me, what eyes hath [[Love\|love]] put in my head<br>O never say that I was false of heart<br>O that you were yourself! But, [[Love\|love]], you are<br>O thou my lovely boy, who in thy power<br>O truant muse, what shall be thy amends<br>Or I shall live your epitaph to make<br>Or whether doth my mind, being crowned with you<br><br>Poor [[Soul\|soul]], the centre of my sinful earth<br><br>Say that thou didst forsake me for some fault<br>Shall I compare thee to a [[Summer\|summer]]’s day?<br>Sin of [[Self\|self]]-[[Love\|love]] possesseth all mine eye<br>Since brass, nor stone, nor earth, nor boundless sea<br>Since I left you mine eye is in my mind<br>So am I as the rich whose blessed key<br>So are you to my thoughts as food to life<br>So is it not with me as with that muse<br>So, now I have confessed that he is thine<br>So oft have I invoked thee for my muse<br>So shall I live supposing thou art true<br>Some glory in their birth, some in their skill<br>Some say thy fault is youth, some wantonness<br>Sweet [[Love\|love]], renew thy force. Be it not said<br><br>Take all my loves, my [[Love\|love]], yea, take them all<br>That god forbid, that made me first your slave<br>That thou are blamed shall not be thy defect<br>That thou hast her, it is not all my grief<br>That [[Time\|time]] of year thou mayst in me behold<br>That you were once unkind befriends me now<br>The forward violet thus did I chide<br>The little [[Love\|love]]-god lying once asleep<br>The other two, slight air and purging [[Fire\|fire]]<br>Then hate me when thou wilt, if ever, now<br>Then let not [[Winter\|winter]]’s ragged hand deface<br>Th’expense of spirit in a waste of shame<br>They that have power to hurt and will do none<br>Thine eyes I [[Love\|love]], and they, as pitying me<br>Those hours that with gentle work did frame<br>Those lines that I before have writ do lie<br>Those lips that [[Love\|love]]’s own hand did make<br>Those parts of thee that the world’s eye doth view<br>Those pretty wrongs that liberty commits<br>Thou art as tyrannous so as thou art<br>Thou blind [[Fool\|fool]] [[Love\|love]], what dost thou to mine eyes<br>Thus can my [[Love\|love]] excuse the slow offence<br>Thus is his cheek the map of days outworn<br>Thy bosom is endeared with all hearts<br>Thy gift, thy tables, are within my brain<br>Thy glass will show thee how thy beauties wear<br>Tired with all these, for restful [[Death\|death]] I cry<br>’Tis better to be vile than vile esteemed<br>To me, fair friend, you never can be old<br>Two loves I have, of comfort and [[Despair\|despair]]<br><br>Unthrifty loveliness, why dost thou spend<br><br>Was it the proud full sail of his great verse<br>Weary with toil I haste me to my bed<br>Were’t aught to me I bore the canopy<br>What is your substance, whereof are you made<br>What potions have I drunk of siren [[Tears\|tears]]<br>What’s in the brain that ink may character<br>When forty winters shall besiege thy brow<br>When I consider every thing that grows<br>When I do count the [[Clock\|clock]] that tells the [[Time\|time]]<br>When I have seen by [[Time\|time]]’s fell hand defaced<br>When, in disgrace with fortune and men’s eyes<br>When in the chronicle of wasted [[Time\|time]]<br>When most I wink, then do mine eyes best see<br>When my [[Love\|love]] swears that she is made of [[Truth\|truth]]<br>When thou shalt be disposed to set me light<br>When to the sessions of sweet silent thought<br>Where art thou, muse, that thou forget’st so long<br>Whilst I alonecall upon thy aid<br>Who is it that says most which can say more<br>Who will believe my verse in [[Time\|time]] to come<br>Whoever hath her wish, thou hast thy Will<br>Why didst thou promise such a beauteous day<br>Why is my verse so barren of new [[Pride\|pride]]<br><br>Your [[Love\|love]] and pity doth th‘impression fill |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_2" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act I, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Palamon]] and |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Arcite]] |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Dear [[Palamon]], dearer in [[Love\|love]] than [[Blood\|blood]],<br>And our prime cousin, yet unhardened in<br>The crimes of [[Nature\|nature]], let us leave the [[City\|city]],<br>Thebes, and the temptings in’t, before we further<br>Sully our gloss of youth.<br>And here to keep in abstinence we shame<br>As in incontinence; for not to swim<br>I’th’ aid o’th’ current were almost to sink—<br>At least to frustrate striving; and to follow<br>The common stream ’twould bring us to an eddy<br>Where we should turn or drown; if labour through,<br>Our gain but life and weakness. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Your advice<br>Is cried up with example. What strange ruins<br>Since first we went to school may we perceive<br>Walking in Thebes? Scars and bare weeds<br>The gain o’th’ martialist who did propound<br>To his bold ends [[Honour\|honour]] and golden ingots,<br>Which though he won, he had not; and now flirted<br>By peace for whom he fought. Who then shall offer<br>To Mars’s so-scorned altar? I do bleed<br>When such I meet, and wish great Juno would<br>Resume her ancient fit of [[Jealousy\|jealousy]]<br>To get the soldier work, that peace might purge<br>For her repletion and retain anew<br>Her charitable heart, now hard and harsher<br>Than strife or war could be. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Are you not out?<br>Meet you no ruin but the soldier in<br>The cranks and turns of Thebes? You did begin<br>As if you met decays of many kinds.<br>Perceive you none that do arouse your pity<br>But th’unconsidered soldier? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes, I pity<br>Decays where’er I find them, but such most<br>That, sweating in an honourable toil,<br>Are paid with ice to cool ’em. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | ’Tis not this<br>I did begin to speak of. This is virtue,<br>Of no respect in Thebes. I spake of Thebes,<br>How dangerous, if we will keep our honours,<br>It is for our residing where every evil<br>Hath a good colour, where every seeming good’s<br>A certain evil, where not to be ev’n jump<br>As they are here were to be strangers, and<br>Such things to be, mere monsters. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | ’Tis in our power,<br>Unless we [[Fear\|fear]] that apes can tutor’s, to<br>Be masters of our manners. What need I<br>Affect another’s gait, which is not catching<br>Where there is [[Faith\|faith]]? Or to be fond upon<br>Another’s way of speech, when by mine own<br>I may be reasonably conceived—saved, too—<br>Speaking it truly? Why am I bound<br>By any generous bond to follow him<br>Follows his tailor, haply so long until<br>The followed make pursuit? Or let me know<br>Why mine own barber is unblest—with him<br>My poor chin, too—for ’tis not scissored just<br>To such a favourite’s glass? What canon is there<br>That does command my rapier from my hip<br>To dangle’t in my hand? Or to go tiptoe<br>Before the street be foul? Either I am<br>The fore-[[Horse\|horse]] in the team or I am none<br>That draw i’th’ sequent trace. These poor slight<br>sores<br>Need not a plantain. That which rips my bosom<br>Almost to th’ heart’s— |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Our uncle Creon. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | He,<br>A most unbounded [[Tyrant\|tyrant]], whose successes<br>Makes heaven unfeared and villainy assured<br>Beyond its power there’s nothing; almost puts<br>[[Faith]] in a fever, and deifies [[Alone\|alone]]<br>Voluble chance; who only attributes<br>The faculties of other instruments<br>To his own nerves and act; commands men’s service,<br>And what they win in’t, boot and glory; one<br>That fears not to do harm, good dares not. Let<br>The [[Blood\|blood]] of mine that’s sib to him be sucked<br>From me with leeches. Let them break and fall<br>Off me with that corruption. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Clear-spirited cousin,<br>Let’s leave his [[Court\|court]] that we may nothing share<br>Of his loud infamy: for our milk<br>Will relish of the pasture, and we must<br>Be vile or disobedient; not his kinsmen<br>In [[Blood\|blood]] unless in quality. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Nothing truer.<br>I think the echoes of his shames have deafed<br>The ears of heav’nly justice. Widows’ cries<br>Descend again into their throats and have not |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Valerius |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Due audience of the gods—Valerius. |
| VALERIUS | The [[King]] calls for you; yet be leaden-footed<br>Till his great rage be off him. Phoebus, when<br>He broke his whipstock and exclaimed against<br>The horses of the sun, but whispered to<br>The loudness of his fury. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Small winds shake him.<br>But what’s the matter? |
| VALERIUS | [[Theseus]], who where he threats, appals, hath sent<br>Deadly defiance to him and pronounces<br>Ruin to Thebes, who is at hand to seal<br>The promise of his wrath. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Let him approach.<br>But that we [[Fear\|fear]] the gods in him, he brings not<br>A jot of terror to us. Yet what man<br>Thirds his own worth—the case is each of ours—<br>When that his action’s dregged with mind assured<br>’Tis bad he goes about. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Leave that unreasoned.<br>Our services stand now for Thebes, not Creon,<br>Yet to be neutral to him were dishonour,<br>Rebellious to oppose. Therefore we must<br>With him stand to the [[Mercy\|mercy]] of our fate,<br>Who hath bounded our last minute. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | So we must.<br>Is’t said this war’s afoot? Or it shall be<br>On fail of some condition? |
| VALERIUS | ’Tis in motion,<br>The intelligence of state came in the instant<br>With the defier. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Let’s to the [[King]], who, were he A quarter carrier of that [[Honour\|honour]] which<br>His enemy come in, the [[Blood\|blood]] we venture<br>Should be as for our health, which were not spent,<br>Rather laid out for purchase. But, alas,<br>Our hands advanced before our hearts, what will<br>The fall o’th’ stroke do damage? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Let th’event—That never-erring arbitrator—tell us<br>When we know all ourselves, and let us follow<br>The becking of our chance. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_3" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act I, Scene 3" data-chapter="Scene 3" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Pirithous, [[Hippolyta]], and [[Emilia]] |
| PIRITHOUS | No further. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Sir, farewell. Repeat my wishes To our great lord, of whose [[success]] I dare not<br>Make any timorous question; yet I wish him<br>Excess and overflow of power, an’t might be,<br>To dure ill-dealing fortune. [[Speed]] to him;<br>Store never hurts good governors. |
| PIRITHOUS | Though I know His ocean needs not my poor drops, yet they<br>Must yield their tribute there. (To [[Emilia]]) My precious<br>maid,<br>Those best affections that the heavens infuse<br>In their best-tempered pieces keep enthroned<br>In your dear heart. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Thanks, sir. Remember me To our all-royal brother, for whose [[Speed\|speed]]<br>The great Bellona I’ll solicit; and<br>Since in our terrene state petitions are not<br>Without gifts understood, I’ll offer to her<br>What I shall be advised she likes. Our hearts<br>Are in his army, in his tent. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | In’s bosom.<br>We have been soldiers, and we cannot weep<br>When our friends don their helms, or put to sea,<br>Or tell of babes broached on the lance, or women<br>That have sod their infants in—and after eat them—<br>The brine they wept at killing ’em: then if<br>You stay to see of us such spinsters, we<br>Should hold you here forever. |
| PIRITHOUS | Peace be to you As I pursue this war, which shall be then<br>Beyond further requiring. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Pirithous |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | How his [[Longing\|longing]] Follows his friend! Since his depart, his sports,<br>Though craving seriousness and skill, passed slightly<br>His careless execution, where nor gain<br>Made him regard or loss consider, but<br>Playing one business in his hand, another<br>Directing in his head, his mind [[Nurse\|nurse]] equal<br>To these so diff’ring twins. Have you observed him<br>Since our great lord departed? |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | With much labour; And I did [[Love\|love]] him for’t. They two have cabined<br>In many as dangerous as poor a corner,<br>Peril and want contending; they have skiffed<br>Torrents whose roaring tyranny and power<br>I’th’ least of these was dreadful, and they have<br>Fought out together where [[Death\|death]]’s [[Self\|self]] was lodged;<br>Yet fate hath brought them off. Their knot of [[Love\|love]],<br>Tied, weaved, entangled with so true, so long,<br>And with a finger of so deep a cunning,<br>May be outworn, never undone. I think<br>[[Theseus]] cannot be umpire to himself,<br>Cleaving his [[Conscience\|conscience]] into twain and doing<br>Each side like justice, which he loves best. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Doubtless There is a best, and reason has no manners<br>To say it is not you. I was acquainted<br>Once with a [[Time\|time]] when I enjoyed a playfellow;<br>You were at wars when she the [[Grave\|grave]] enriched,<br>Who made too proud the bed; took leave o’th’<br>[[Moon\|moon]]—<br>Which then looked pale at parting—when our count<br>Was each eleven. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | ’Twas Flavina. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Yes.<br>You talk of Pirithous’ and [[Theseus]]’ [[Love\|love]]:<br>Theirs has more ground, is more maturely seasoned,<br>More buckled with strong judgement, and their needs<br>The one of th‘other may be said to [[Water\|water]]<br>Their intertangled roots of [[Love\|love]]; but I<br>And she I sigh and spoke of were things [[Innocent\|innocent]],<br>Loved for we did, and like the elements,<br>That know not what, nor why, yet do effect<br>Rare issues by their operance, our souls<br>Did so to one another. What she liked<br>Was then of me approved; what not, condemned—<br>No more arraignment. The flower that I would pluck<br>And put between my breasts—O then but beginning<br>To swell about the blossom—she would long<br>Till she had such another, and commit it<br>To the like [[Innocent\|innocent]] cradle, where, phoenix-like,<br>They died in perfume. On my head no toy<br>But was her pattern. Her affections—pretty,<br>Though happily her careless wear—I followed<br>For my most serious decking. Had mine ear<br>Stol’n some new air, or at adventure hummed one,<br>From musical coinage, why, it was a note<br>Whereon her spirits would sojourn—rather dwell on—<br>And sing it in her slumbers. This rehearsal—<br>Which, seely [[innocence]] wots [[Well\|well]], comes in<br>Like old emportment’s bastard—has this end:<br>That the true [[Love\|love]] ’tween maid and maid may be<br>More than in sex dividual. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | You’re out of breath, And this high-speeded pace is but to say<br>That you shall never, like the maid Flavina,<br>[[Love]] any that’s called man. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | I am sure I shall not. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Now alack, weak sister, I must no more believe thee in this point—<br>Though in’t I know thou dost believe thyself—<br>Than I will trust a sickly appetite<br>That loathes even as it longs. But sure, my sister,<br>If I were ripe for your [[Persuasion\|persuasion]], you<br>Have said enough to shake me from the arm<br>Of the all-noble [[Theseus]], for whose fortunes<br>I will now in and kneel, with great assurance<br>That we more than his Pirithous possess<br>The high throne in his heart. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | I am not<br>Against your [[Faith\|faith]], yet I continue mine. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_4" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act I, Scene 4" data-chapter="Scene 4" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Cornetts. A battle struck within. Then a retreat. Flourish. Then enter [[Theseus]], victor. The three Queens meet him and fall on their faces before him. Also enter a Herald, and attendants bearing [[Palamon]] and [[Arcite]] on two hearses |
| FIRST [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[Theseus]])<br>To thee no star be dark. |
| SECOND [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[Theseus]]) Both heaven and earth<br>Friend thee for ever. |
| THIRD [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[Theseus]]) All the good that may Be wished upon thy head, I cry ‘Amen’ to’t. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Th’impartial gods, who from the mounted heavens<br>View us their mortal herd, behold who err<br>And in their [[Time\|time]] chastise. Go and find out<br>The bones of your dead lords and [[Honour\|honour]] them<br>With treble ceremony: rather than a gap<br>Should be in their dear rites we would supply’t.<br>But those we will depute which shall invest<br>You in your dignities, and even each thing<br>Our haste does leave imperfect. So adieu,<br>And heaven’s good eyes look on you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the Queens |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | What are those? |
| HERALD | Men of great quality, as may be judged<br>By their appointment. Some of Thebes have told’s<br>They are sisters’ children, nephews to the [[King]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | By th’ helm of Mars I saw them in the war,<br>Like to a pair of lions smeared with prey,<br>Make lanes in troops aghast. I fixed my note<br>Constantly on them, for they were a mark<br>Worth a god’s view. What prisoner was’t that told me<br>When I enquired their names? |
| HERALD | Wi’ leave, they’re called [[Arcite]] and [[Palamon]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ’Tis right: those, those. They are not dead? |
| HERALD | Nor in a state of life. Had they been taken<br>When their last hurts were given, ’twas possible<br>They might have been recovered. Yet they breathe,<br>And have the name of men. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Then like men use ‘em. The very lees of such, millions of rates<br>Exceed the wine of others. All our surgeons<br>Convent in their behoof; our richest balms,<br>Rather than niggard, waste. Their lives concern us<br>Much more than Thebes is worth. Rather than have<br>’em<br>Freed of this plight and in their morning state— |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound and at liberty—I would ’em dead; |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | But forty-thousandfold we had rather have ’em<br>Prisoners to us, than [[Death\|death]]. Bear ‘em speedily<br>From our kind air, to them unkind, and minister<br>What man to man may do-for our sake, more,<br>Since I have known frights, fury, friends’ behests,<br>[[Love]]’s provocations, zeal, a mistress’ task,<br>[[Desire]] of liberty, a fever, [[Madness\|madness]],<br>Hath set a mark which [[Nature\|nature]] could not reach to<br>Without some imposition, sickness in will<br>O’er-wrestling strength in reason. For our [[Love\|love]]<br>And great [[Apollo]]’s [[Mercy\|mercy]], all our best<br>Their best skill tender.—Lead into the [[City\|city]]<br>Where, having bound things scattered, we will post<br>To Athens fore our army. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_5" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act I, Scene 5" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Music. Enter the three Queens with the hearses of their lords in a funeral solemnity, with attendants |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Song]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Urns and odours, bring away,<br>Vapours, sighs, darken the day;<br>Our dole more deadly looks than dying.<br>Balms and gums and heavy cheers,<br>Sacred vials filled with [[Tears\|tears]],<br>And clamours through the wild air flying:<br><br>Come all sad and solemn shows,<br>That are quick-eyed pleasure’s foes.<br>We convent naught else but woes,<br>We convent naught else but woes. |
| THIRD [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | This funeral path brings to your household’s [[Grave\|grave]]—<br>Joy seize on you again, peace [[Sleep\|sleep]] with him. |
| SECOND [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | And this to yours. |
| FIRST [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Yours this way. Heavens lend A thousand differing ways to one sure end. |
| THIRD [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | This world’s a [[City\|city]] full of straying streets,<br>And [[Death\|death]]’s the market-place where each one meets. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_1" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer and the Wooer |
| JAILER | I may depart with little, while I live; something I may cast to you, not much. Alas, the [[Prison\|prison]] I keep, though it be for great ones, yet they seldom come; before one salmon you shall take a number of minnows. I am given out to be better lined than it can appear to me report is a true speaker. I would I were really that I am delivered to be. Marry, what I have—be it what it will—I will assure upon my daughter at the day of my [[Death\|death]]. |
| WOOER | Sir, I demand no more than your own offer, and I will estate your daughter in what I have promised. |
| JAILER | [[Well]], we will talk more of this when the solemnity is past. But have you a full promise of her? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer’s Daughter with rushes |
| JAILER | When that shall be seen, I tender my consent. |
| WOOER | I have, sir. Here she comes. |
| JAILER | (to Daughter) Your friend and I have chanced to name you here, upon the old business—but no more of that now. So soon as the [[Court\|court]] hurry is over we will have an end of it. I’th’ mean [[Time\|time]], look tenderly to the two prisoners. I can tell you they are princes. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | These strewings are for their chamber. ’Tis pity they are in [[Prison\|prison]], and ’twere pity they should be out. I do think they have patience to make any adversity ashamed; the [[Prison\|prison]] itself is proud of ’em, and they have all the world in their chamber. |
| JAILER | They are famed to be a pair of absolute men. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | By my troth, I think fame but stammers ’em—they stand a grece above the reach of report. |
| JAILER | I heard them reported in the battle to be the only doers. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | Nay, most likely, for they are noble sufferers. I marvel how they would have looked had they been victors, that with such a constant nobility enforce a [[Freedom\|freedom]] out of bondage, making misery their mirth, and affliction a toy to jest at. |
| JAILER | Do they so? |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | It seems to me they have no more sense of their captivity than I of ruling Athens. They eat [[Well\|well]], look merrily, discourse of many things, but nothing of their own restraint and disasters. Yet sometime a divided sigh—martyred as ’twere i’th’ deliverance—will break from one of them, when the other presently gives it so sweet a rebuke that I could wish myself a sigh to be so chid, or at least a sigher to be comforted. |
| WOOER | I never saw ’em. |
| JAILER | The Duke himself came privately in the [[Night\|night]], |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Palamon]] and [[Arcite]] appear at a [[Window\|window]] above |
| JAILER | and so did they. What the reason of it is I know not. Look, yonder they are. That’s [[Arcite]] looks out. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | No, sir, no—that’s [[Palamon]]. [[Arcite]] is the lower of the twain—(pointing at [[Arcite]]) you may perceive a part of him. |
| JAILER | Go to, leave your pointing. They would not make us their object. Out of their sight. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | It is a holiday to look on them. Lord, the difference of men! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--01_exchange_dialogue" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — exchange (dialogue) [1]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="exchange"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Palamon]] and [[Arcite]] in [[Prison\|prison]], in shackles, above |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | How do you, noble cousin? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | How do you, sir? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Why, strong enough to laugh at misery And bear the chance of war. Yet we are prisoners, I [[Fear\|fear]], for ever, cousin. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I believe it, And to that destiny have patiently Laid up my hour to come. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | O, cousin [[Arcite]], Where is Thebes now? Where is our noble country? Where are our friends and kindreds? Never more Must we behold those comforts, never see The hardy youths strive for the games of [[Honour\|honour]], Hung with the painted favours of their ladies, Like tall ships under sail; then start amongst ’em And, as an east [[Wind\|wind]], leave ’em all behind us, Like lazy clouds, whilst [[Palamon]] and [[Arcite]], Even in the wagging of a wanton leg, Outstripped the people’s praises, won the garlands Ere they have [[Time\|time]] to wish ‘em ours. O never Shall we two exercise, like twins of [[Honour\|honour]], Our arms again and feel our fiery horses Like proud seas under us. Our good swords, now— Better the red-eyed god of war ne’er wore— Ravished our sides, like age must run to rust And deck the temples of those gods that hate us. These hands shall never draw ’em out like [[Lightning\|lightning]] To blast whole armies more. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--02_monologue_arcite" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — monologue (ARCITE) [2]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="monologue"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | No, [[Palamon]], Those hopes are prisoners with us. Here we are, And here the graces of our youths must wither, Like a too-timely [[spring]]. Here age must find us And, which is heaviest, [[Palamon]], unmarried— The sweet embraces of a loving wife Loaden with kisses, armed with thousand Cupids, Shall never clasp our necks; no issue know us; No figures of ourselves shall we e’er see To glad our age, and, like young eagles, teach ’em Boldly to gaze against bright arms and say, ‘Remember what your fathers were, and conquer.’ The fair-eyed maids shall weep our banishments, And in their [[songs]] curse ever-blinded fortune, Till she for shame see what a wrong she has done To youth and [[Nature\|nature]]. This is all our world. We shall know nothing here but one another, Hear nothing but the [[Clock\|clock]] that tells our woes. The vine shall grow, but we shall never see it; [[Summer]] shall come, and with her all delights, But dead-cold [[Winter\|winter]] must inhabit here still. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--03_exchange_palamon" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — exchange (PALAMON) [3]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="exchange"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | ’Tis too true, [[Arcite]]. To our Theban hounds That shook the aged forest with their echoes, No more now must we holler; no more shake Our pointed javelins whilst the angry swine Flies like a Parthian quiver from our rages, Struck with our [[Well\|well]]-steeled darts. All valiant uses— The food and nourishment of noble minds— In us two here shall perish; we shall die— Which is the curse of [[Honour\|honour]]—lastly, Children of grief and ignorance. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yet, cousin, Even from the [[Bottom\|bottom]] of these miseries, From all that fortune can inflict upon us, I see two comforts rising—two mere blessings, If the gods please, to hold here a brave patience And the enjoying of our griefs together. Whilst [[Palamon]] is with me, let me perish If I think this our [[Prison\|prison]]. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Certainly ’Tis a main goodness, cousin, that our fortunes Were twined together. ’Tis most true, two souls Put in two noble bodies, let ’em suffer The gall of hazard, so they grow together, Will never sink; they must not, say they could. A willing man dies sleeping and all’s done. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Shall we make worthy uses of this place That all men hate so much? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | How, gentle cousin? |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--04_monologue_arcite" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — monologue (ARCITE) [4]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="monologue"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Let’s think this [[Prison\|prison]] holy sanctuary, To keep us from corruption of worse men. We are young, and yet [[Desire\|desire]] the ways of [[Honour\|honour]] That liberty and common conversation, The [[Poison\|poison]] of pure spirits, might, like women, Woo us to wander from. What worthy blessing Can be, but our imaginations May make it ours? And here being thus together, We are an endless mine to one another: We are one another’s wife, ever begetting New births of [[Love\|love]]; we are father, friends, acquaintance; We are in one another, families— I am your heir, and you are mine; this place Is our inheritance: no hard oppressor Dare take this from us. Here, with a little patience, We shall live long and loving. No surfeits seek us— The hand of war hurts none here, nor the seas [[Swallow]] their youth. Were we at liberty A wife might part us lawfully, or business; Quarrels consume us; envy of ill men Crave our acquaintance. I might sicken, cousin, Where you should never know it, and so perish Without your noble hand to close mine eyes, Or prayers to the gods. A thousand chances, Were we from hence, would sever us. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--05_exchange_palamon" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — exchange (PALAMON) [5]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="exchange"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | You have made me—I thank you, cousin [[Arcite]]—almost wanton With my captivity. What a misery It is to live abroad, and everywhere! ’Tis like a beast, methinks. I find the [[Court\|court]] here; I am sure, a more content; and all those pleasures That woo the wills of men to [[Vanity\|vanity]] I see through now, and am sufficient To tell the world ’tis but a gaudy [[Shadow\|shadow]], That old [[Time]], as he passes by, takes with him. What had we been, old in the [[Court\|court]] of Creon, Where sin is justice, lust and ignorance The virtues of the great ones? Cousin [[Arcite]], Had not the loving gods found this place for us, We had died as they do, ill old men, unwept, And had their epitaphs, the people’s curses. Shall I say more? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I would hear you still. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Ye shall. Is there record of any two that loved Better than we do, [[Arcite]]? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Sure there cannot. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | I do not think it possible our [[Friendship\|friendship]] Should ever leave us. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Till our deaths it cannot, Enter [[Emilia]] and her Woman below. [[Palamon]] sees [[Emilia]] and is silent And after [[Death\|death]] our spirits shall be led To those that [[Love\|love]] eternally. Speak on, sir. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman) This [[Garden\|garden]] has a world of pleasure in’t. What flower is this? |
| WOMAN | ’Tis called narcissus, madam. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | That was a fair boy, certain, but a [[Fool\|fool]] To [[Love\|love]] himself. Were there not maids enough? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[Palamon]]) Pray forward. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman) Or were they all hard-hearted? |
| WOMAN | They could not be to one so fair. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Thou wouldst not. |
| WOMAN | I think I should not, madam. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | That’s a good wench—But take heed to your kindness, though. |
| WOMAN | Why, madam? |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Men are mad things. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[Palamon]]) Will ye go forward, cousin? |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman) Canst not thou work such [[Flowers\|flowers]] in silk, wench? |
| WOMAN | Yes. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | I’ll have a gown full of ’em, and of these. This is a pretty colour—will’t not do Rarely upon a skirt, wench? |
| WOMAN | Dainty, madam. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[Palamon]]) Cousin, cousin, how do you, sir? Why, [[Palamon]]! |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Never till now was I in [[Prison\|prison]], [[Arcite]]. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Why, what’s the matter, man? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Behold and [[Wonder\|wonder]]! [[Arcite]] sees [[Emilia]] By heaven, she is a goddess! |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Ha! |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Do reverence. She is a goddess, [[Arcite]]. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman) Of all [[Flowers\|flowers]] Methinks a [[Rose\|rose]] is best. |
| WOMAN | Why, gentle madam? |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | It is the very emblem of a maid— For when the west [[Wind\|wind]] courts her gently, How modestly she blows, and paints the sun With her chaste blushes! When the north comes near her, Rude and impatient, then, like chastity, She locks her beauties in her bud again, And [[Leaves\|leaves]] him to base briers. |
| WOMAN | Yet, good madam, Sometimes her modesty will blow so far She falls for’t—a maid, If she have any [[Honour\|honour]], would be loath To take example by her. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Thou art wanton. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[Palamon]]) She is wondrous fair. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | She is all the [[Beauty\|beauty]] extant. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman) The sun grows high—let’s walk in. Keep these [[Flowers\|flowers]]. We’ll see how close art can come near their colours. I am wondrous merry-hearted—I could laugh now. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--06_exchange_woman" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — exchange (WOMAN) [6]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="exchange"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| WOMAN | I could lie down, I am sure. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | And take one with you? |
| WOMAN | That’s as we bargain, madam. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | [[Well]], agree then. Exeunt [[Emilia]] and her Woman |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | What think you of this [[Beauty\|beauty]]? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | ’Tis a rare one. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Is’t but a rare one? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yes, a matchless [[Beauty\|beauty]]. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Might not a man [[Well\|well]] lose himself and [[Love\|love]] her? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I cannot tell what you have done; I have, Beshrew mine eyes for’t. Now I feel my shackles. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | You [[Love\|love]] her then? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Who would not? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | And [[Desire\|desire]] her? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Before my liberty. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | I saw her first. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | That’s nothing. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | But it shall be. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I saw her too. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes, but you must not [[Love\|love]] her. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I will not, as you do, to worship her As she is heavenly and a blessèd goddess! I [[Love\|love]] her as a woman, to enjoy her— So both may [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | You shall not [[Love\|love]] at all. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not [[Love\|love]] at all—who shall deny me? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | I that first saw her, I that took possession First with mine eye of all those beauties In her revealed to mankind. If thou lov’st her, Or entertain’st a [[Hope\|hope]] to blast my wishes, Thou art a traitor, [[Arcite]], and a fellow False as thy title to her. [[Friendship]], [[Blood\|blood]], And all the ties between us I disclaim, If thou once think upon her. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yes, I [[Love\|love]] her—And if the lives of all my name lay on it, I must do so. I [[Love\|love]] her with my [[Soul\|soul]]— If that will lose ye, farewell, [[Palamon]]! I say again, I [[Love\|love]] her, and in loving her maintain I am as worthy and as free a [[Lover\|lover]], And have as just a title to her [[Beauty\|beauty]], As any [[Palamon]], or any living That is a man’s son. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Have I called thee friend? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yes, and have found me so. Why are you moved thus? Let me deal coldly with you. Am not I Part of your [[Blood\|blood]], part of your [[Soul\|soul]]? You have told me That I was [[Palamon]] and you were [[Arcite]]. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Am not I liable to those affections, Those joys, griefs, angers, fears, my friend shall suffer? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Ye may be. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Why then would you deal so cunningly, So strangely, so unlike a noble kinsman, To [[Love\|love]] [[Alone\|alone]]? Speak truly. Do you think me Unworthy of her sight? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | No, but unjust If thou pursue that sight. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Because another First sees the enemy, shall I stand still, And let mine [[Honour\|honour]] down, and never charge? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes, if he be but one. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | But say that one Had rather combat me? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Let that one say so, And use thy [[Freedom\|freedom]]; else, if thou pursuest her, Be as that cursed man that hates his country, A branded [[Villain\|villain]]. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | You are mad. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | I must be. Till thou art worthy, [[Arcite]], it concerns me; And in this [[Madness\|madness]] if I hazard thee And take thy life, I deal but truly. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Fie, sir. You play the [[Child\|child]] extremely. I will [[Love\|love]] her, I must, I ought to do so, and I dare— And all this justly. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | O, that now, that now Thy false [[Self\|self]] and thy friend had but this fortune— To be one hour at liberty and grasp Our good swords in our hands! I would quickly teach thee What ’twere to filch affection from another. Thou art baser in it than a cutpurse. Put but thy head out of this [[Window\|window]] more And, as I have a [[Soul\|soul]], I’ll nail thy life to’t. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--07_exchange_arcite" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — exchange (ARCITE) [7]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="exchange"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Thou dar’st not, [[Fool\|fool]]; thou canst not; thou art feeble. Put my head out? I’ll throw my [[Body\|body]] out And leap the [[Garden\|garden]] when I see her next, Enter the Jailer above And pitch between her arms to anger thee. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | No more—the keeper’s coming. I shall live To knock thy brains out with my shackles. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Do. |
| JAILER | By your leave, gentlemen. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Now, honest keeper? |
| JAILER | Lord [[Arcite]], you must presently to th’ Duke. The cause I know not yet. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I am ready, keeper. |
| JAILER | Prince [[Palamon]], I must a while bereave you Of your fair cousin’s company. Exeunt [[Arcite]] and the Jailer |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--08_monologue_palamon" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — monologue (PALAMON) [8]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="monologue"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | And me, too, Even when you please, of life. Why is he sent for? It may be he shall marry her—he’s goodly, And like enough the Duke hath taken notice Both of his [[Blood\|blood]] and [[Body\|body]]. But his falsehood! Why should a friend be treacherous? If that Get him a wife so noble and so fair, Let honest men ne’er [[Love\|love]] again. Once more I would but see this fair one. Blessèd [[Garden\|garden]], And fruit and [[Flowers\|flowers]] more blessed, that still blossom As her bright eyes shine on ye! Would I were, For all the fortune of my life hereafter, Yon little tree, yon blooming apricot— How I would spread and fling my wanton arms In at her [[Window\|window]]! I would bring her fruit Fit for the gods to feed on; youth and pleasure Still as she tasted should be doubled on her; And if she be not heavenly, I would make her So near the gods in [[Nature\|nature]] they should [[Fear\|fear]] her— Enter the Jailer bove And then I am sure she would [[Love\|love]] me. How now, keeper, Where’s [[Arcite]]? |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2--09_exchange_jailer" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 2 — exchange (JAILER) [9]" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="exchange"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| JAILER | Banished—Prince Pirithous Obtained his liberty; but never more, Upon his oath and life, must he set foot Upon this kingdom. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | aside He’s a blessed man. He shall see Thebes again, and call to arms The bold young men that, when he bids ’em charge, Fall on like [[Fire\|fire]]. [[Arcite]] shall have a fortune, If he dare make himself a worthy [[Lover\|lover]], Yet in the field to strike a battle for her; And if he lose her then, he’s a cold coward. How bravely may he bear himself to win her If he be noble [[Arcite]]; thousand ways! Were I at liberty I would do things Of such a virtuous [[Greatness\|greatness]] that this lady, This blushing virgin, should take manhood to her And seek to ravish me. |
| JAILER | My lord, for you I have this charge to— |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | To discharge my life. |
| JAILER | No, but from this place to remove your lordship— The windows are too open. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Devils take ’em That are so envious to me—prithee kill me. |
| JAILER | And hang for’t afterward? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | By this good light, Had I as word I would kill thee. |
| JAILER | Why, my lord? |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Thou bring’st such pelting scurvy news continually, Thou art not worthy life. I will not go. |
| JAILER | Indeed you must, my lord. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | May I see the [[Garden\|garden]]? |
| JAILER | No. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Then I am resolved—I will not go. |
| JAILER | I must constrain you, then; and for you are dangerous, I’ll clap more irons on you. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Do, good keeper. I’ll shake ’em so ye shall not [[Sleep\|sleep]]: I’ll make ye a new morris. Must I go? |
| JAILER | There is no remedy. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Farewell, kind [[Window\|window]]. May rude [[Wind\|wind]] never hurt thee. O, my lady, If ever thou hast felt what sorrow was, Dream how I suffer. Come, now bury me. Exeunt [[Palamon]] and the Jailer |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_3" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 3" data-chapter="Scene 3" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Arcite]] |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Banished the kingdom? ’Tis a benefit,<br>A [[Mercy\|mercy]] I must thank ’em for; but banished<br>The free enjoying of that face I die for—<br>O, ‘twas a studied punishment, a [[Death\|death]]<br>Beyond [[Imagination\|imagination]]; such a vengeance<br>That, were I old and wicked, all my sins<br>Could never pluck upon me. [[Palamon]],<br>Thou hast the start now—thou shalt stay and see<br>Her bright eyes break each morning ’gainst thy<br>[[Window\|window]],<br>And let in life into thee. Thou shalt feed<br>Upon the sweetness of a noble [[Beauty\|beauty]]<br>That [[Nature\|nature]] ne’er exceeded, nor ne’er shall.<br>Good gods! What happiness has [[Palamon]]!<br>Twenty to one he’ll come to speak to her,<br>And if she be as gentle as she’s fair,<br>I know she’s his—he has a tongue will tame<br>Tempests and make the wild rocks wanton.<br>Come what can come,<br>The worst is [[Death\|death]]. I will not leave the kingdom.<br>I know mine own is but a heap of ruins,<br>And no redress there. If I go he has her.<br>I am resolved another shape shall make me,<br>Or end my fortunes. Either way I am happy—<br>I’ll see her and be near her, or no more. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter four Country People, one of whom carries a garland before them. [[Arcite]] stands apart |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | My masters, I’ll be there—that’s certain. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | And I’ll be there. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | And I. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Why then, have with ye, boys! ’Tis but a chiding—<br>Let the plough play today, I’ll tickle’t out<br>Of the jades’ tails tomorrow. |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | I am sure<br>To have my wife as jealous as a turkey—<br>But that’s all one. I’ll go through, let her mumble. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | Clap her aboard tomorrow [[Night\|night]] and stow her,<br>And all’s made up again. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | Ay, do but put A fescue in her fist and you shall see her<br>Take a new lesson out and be a good wench.<br>Do we all hold against the maying? |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Hold? What should ail us? |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | Areas will be there. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | And Sennois, and Rycas, and three better lads ne’er danced under green tree; and ye know what wenches, ha? But will the dainty dominie, the schoolmaster, keep touch, do you think? For he does all, ye know. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | He’ll eat a hornbook ere he fail. Go to, the matter’s too far driven between him and the tanner’s daughter to let slip now, and she must see the Duke, and she must [[Dance\|dance]] too. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Shall we be lusty? |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | All the boys in Athens blow [[Wind\|wind]] i’th’ breech on’s! And here I’ll be and there I’ll be, for our town, and here again and there again—ha, boys, hey for the weavers! |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | This must be done i’th’ woods. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | O, pardon me. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | By any means, our thing of learning said so; where he himself will edify the Duke most parlously in our behalfs—he’s excellent i’th’ woods, bring him to th’ plains, his learning makes no cry. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | We’ll see the sports, then every man to’s tackle—and, sweet companions, let’s rehearse, by any means, before the ladies see us, and do sweetly, and God knows what may come on’t. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Content—the sports once ended, we’ll perform. Away boys, and hold. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | (coming forward) By your [[Leaves\|leaves]], honest friends, pray you whither go you? 6 |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Whither? Why, what a question’s that? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yet ’tis a question<br>To me that know not. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | To the games, my friend. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | Where were you bred, you know it not? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not far, sir<br>Are there such games today? |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | Yes, marry, are there, And such as you never saw. The Duke himself<br>Will be in person there. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | What pastimes are they? |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | Wrestling and running. (To the others) ’Tis a pretty fellow. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | (to [[Arcite]])<br>Thou wilt not go along? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not yet, sir. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | [[Well]], sir, Take your own [[Time\|time]]. (To the others) Come, boys. |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | My mind misgives me—This fellow has a vengeance trick o’th’ hip:<br>Mark how his [[Body\|body]]’s made for’t. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | I’ll be hanged though If he dare venture; hang him, plum porridge!<br>He wrestle? He roast eggs! Come, let’s be gone, lads. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the four Countrymen |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | This is an offered opportunity<br>I durst not wish for. [[Well]] I could have wrestled—<br>The best men called it excellent—and run<br>Swifter than [[Wind\|wind]] upon a field of corn,<br>Curling the wealthy ears, never flew. I’ll venture,<br>And in some poor disguise be there. Who knows<br>Whether my brows may not be girt with garlands,<br>And happiness prefer me to a place<br>Where I may ever dwell in sight of her? Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_4" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 4" data-chapter="Scene 4" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer’s Daughter |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | Why should I [[Love\|love]] this gentleman? ’Tis odds<br>He never will affect me. I am base,<br>My father the mean keeper of his [[Prison\|prison]],<br>And he a prince. To marry him is hopeless,<br>To be his whore is witless. Out upon’t,<br>What pushes are we wenches driven to<br>When fifteen once has found us? First, I saw him;<br>I, seeing, thought he was a goodly man;<br>He has as much to please a woman in him—<br>If he please to bestow it so—as ever<br>These eyes yet looked on. Next, I pitied him,<br>And so would any young wench, o‘my [[Conscience\|conscience]],<br>That ever dreamed or vowed her maidenhead<br>To a young handsome man. Then, I loved him,<br>Extremely loved him, infinitely loved him—<br>And yet he had a cousin fair as he, too.<br>But in my heart was [[Palamon]], and there,<br>Lord, what a coil he keeps! To hear him<br>Sing in an evening, what a heaven it is!<br>And yet his [[songs]] are sad ones. Fairer spoken<br>Was never gentleman. When I come in<br>To bring him [[Water\|water]] in a morning, first<br>He bows his noble [[Body\|body]], then salutes me, thus:<br>‘Fair, gentle maid, good morrow. May thy goodness<br>Get thee a happy husband.’ Once he kissed me—<br>I loved my lips the better ten days after.<br>Would he would do so every day! He grieves much,<br>And me as much to see his misery.<br>What should I do to make him know I [[Love\|love]] him?<br>For I would fain enjoy him. Say I ventured<br>To set him free? What says the law then? Thus much<br>For law or kindred! I will do it,<br>And this [[Night\|night]]; ere tomorrow he shall [[Love\|love]] me. Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_5" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 5" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Short flourish of cornetts and shouts within. Enter [[Theseus]], [[Hippolyta]], Pirithous, [[Emilia]], [[Arcite]] disguised, with a garland, and attendants |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | You have done worthily. I have not seen<br>Since Hercules a man of tougher sinews.<br>Whate’er you are, you run the best and wrestle<br>That these times can allow. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I am proud to please you. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | What country bred you? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | This—but far off, prince. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Are you a gentleman? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | My father said so, And to those gentle uses gave me life. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Are you his heir? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | His youngest, sir. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Your father Sure is a happy sire, then. What proves you? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | A little of all noble qualities.<br>I could have kept a hawk and [[Well\|well]] have hollered<br>To a deep cry of [[dogs]]; I dare not praise<br>My feat in horsemanship, yet they that knew me<br>Would say it was my best piece; last and greatest,<br>I would be thought a soldier. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | You are perfect. |
| PIRITHOIIS | Upon my [[Soul\|soul]], a proper man. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | He is so. |
| PIRITHOUS | (to [[Hippolyta]])<br>How do you like him, lady? |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | I admire him. I have not seen so young a man so noble—<br>If he say true—of his sort. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | Believe His mother was a wondrous handsome woman—<br>His face methinks goes that way. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | But his [[Body\|body]] And fiery mind illustrate a brave father. |
| PIRITHOUS | Mark how his virtue, like a hidden sun,<br>Breaks through his baser garments. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | He’s [[Well\|well]] got, sure. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[Arcite]])<br>What made you seek this place, sir? |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Noble [[Theseus]], To purchase name and do my ablest service<br>To such a [[Well\|well]]-found [[Wonder\|wonder]] as thy worth,<br>For only in thy [[Court\|court]] of all the world<br>Dwells fair-eyed [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| PIRITHOUS | All his words are worthy. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[Arcite]])<br>Sir, we are much indebted to your travel,<br>Nor shall you lose your wish.—Pirithous,<br>Dispose of this fair gentleman. |
| PIRITHOUS | Thanks, [[Theseus]]. (To [[Arcite]]) Whate’er you are, you’re mine, and I shall<br>give you<br>To a most noble service, to this lady,<br>This bright young virgin; pray observe her goodness.<br>You have honoured her fair birthday with your<br>virtues,<br>And as your due you’re hers. Kiss her fair hand, sir. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Sir, you’re a noble giver. (To [[Emilia]]) Dearest [[Beauty\|beauty]],<br>Thus let me seal my vowed [[Faith\|faith]].<br>He kisses her hand<br>When your servant,<br>Your most unworthy creature, but offends you,<br>Command him die, he shall. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | That were too cruel. If you deserve [[Well\|well]], sir, I shall soon see’t.<br>You’re mine, and somewhat better than your rank I’ll<br>use you. |
| PIRITHOUS | (to [[Arcite]])<br>I’ll see you furnished, and, because you say<br>You are a horseman, I must needs entreat you<br>This afternoon to ride—but ’tis a rough one. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | I like him better, prince—I shall not then<br>Freeze in my saddle. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[Hippolyta]]) |
| *(didascalia)* | Sweet, you must be ready—And you, [[Emilia]], to Pirithous] and you, friend—and |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | all,<br>Tomorrow by the sun, to do observance<br>To flow’ry May in Dian’s wood. (To [[Arcite]]) Wait [[Well\|well]],<br>sir,<br>Upon your mistress.—Emity, I [[Hope\|hope]]<br>He shall not go afoot. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | That were a shame, sir, While I have horses. (To [[Arcite]]) Take your choice, and<br>what<br>You want, at any [[Time\|time]], let me but know it.<br>If you serve faithfully, I dare assure you,<br>You’ll find a loving mistress. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | If I do not, Let me find that my father ever hated—<br>Disgrace and blows. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Go, lead the way—you have won it. It shall be so: you shall receive all dues<br>Fit for the [[Honour\|honour]] you have won. ’Twere wrong else.<br>(To [[Emilia]]) Sister, beshrew my heart, you have a<br>servant<br>That, if I were a woman, would be master.<br>But you are wise. |
| [[Emilia\|EMILIA]] | I [[Hope\|hope]] too wise for that, sir. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_6" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act II, Scene 6" data-chapter="Scene 6" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the jailer’s Daughter |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | Let all the dukes and all the devils roar—<br>He is at liberty! I have ventured for him,<br>And out I have brought him. To a little wood<br>A mile hence I have sent him, where a cedar<br>Higher than all the rest spreads like a plane,<br>Fast by a brook—and there he shall keep close<br>Till I provide him files and food, for yet<br>His iron bracelets are not off. O [[Love]],<br>What a stout-hearted [[Child\|child]] thou art! My father<br>Durst better have endured cold iron than done it.<br>I [[Love\|love]] him beyond [[Love\|love]] and beyond reason<br>Or wit or safety. I have made him know it—<br>I care not, I am desperate. If the law<br>Find me and then condemn me for‘t, some wenches,<br>Some honest-hearted maids, will sing my [[Dirge\|dirge]]<br>And tell to [[Memory\|memory]] my [[Death\|death]] was noble,<br>Dying almost a martyr. That way he takes,<br>I purpose, is my way too. Sure, he cannot<br>Be so unmanly as to leave me here.<br>If he do, maids will not so easily<br>Trust men again. And yet, he has not thanked me<br>For what I have done—no, not so much as kissed me—<br>And that, methinks, is not so [[Well\|well]]. Nor scarcely<br>Could I persuade him to become a free man,<br>He made such scruples of the wrong he did<br>To me and to my father. Yet, I [[Hope\|hope]]<br>When he considers more, this [[Love\|love]] of mine<br>Will take more root within him. Let him do<br>What he will with me—so he use me kindly.<br>For use me, so he shall, or I’ll proclaim him,<br>And to his face, no man. I’ll presently<br>Provide him necessaries and pack my clothes up,<br>And where there is a patch of ground I’ll venture,<br>So he be with me. By him, like a [[Shadow\|shadow]],<br>I’ll ever dwell. Within this hour the hubbub<br>Will be all o’er the [[Prison\|prison]]—I am then<br>Kissing the man they look for. Farewell, father:<br>Get many more such prisoners and such daughters,<br>And shortly you may keep yourself. Now to him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_3--scene_1" data-title="The Two Noble Kinsmen — Act III, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A bush in place. Cornetts in sundry places. Noise and hollering as of people a-Maying. Enter [[Arcite]] |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | The Duke has lost Hippo)yta—each took<br>A several laund. This is a solemn rite<br>They owe bloomed May, and the Athenians pay it<br>To th’ heart of ceremony. O, [[Queen]] [[Emilia]],<br>Fresher than May, sweeter<br>Than her gold buttons on the boughs, or all<br>Th’enamelled knacks o’th’ mead or [[Garden\|garden]]—yea,<br>We challenge too the bank of any nymph<br>That makes the stream seem [[Flowers\|flowers]]; thou, O [[Jewel\|jewel]]<br>O’th’ wood, o’th’ world, hast likewise blessed a pace |
| *(didascalia)* | With thy sole presence in thy |
| *(didascalia)* |  rumination |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | That I, poor man, might eftsoons come between<br>And chop on some cold thought. Thrice blessèd<br>chance<br>To drop on such a mistress, expectation<br>Most guiltless on‘t! Tell me, O Lady Fortune,<br>Next after Emily my sovereign, how far<br>I may be proud. She takes strong note of me,<br>Hath made me near her, and this beauteous morn,<br>The prim’st of all the year, presents me with<br>A brace of horses—two such steeds might [[Well\|well]]<br>Be by a pair of kings backed, in a field<br>That their crowns’ titles tried. Alas, alas,<br>Poor cousin [[Palamon]], poor prisoner—thou<br>So little dream’st upon my fortune that<br>Thou think’st thyself the happier thing to be<br>So near [[Emilia]]. Me thou deem’st at Thebes,<br>And therein wretched, although free. But if<br>Thou knew’st my mistress breathed on me, and that<br>I eared her language, lived in her eye-O, coz,<br>What passion would enclose thee! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Palamon]] as out of a bush with his shackles. He bends his fist at [[Arcite]] |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Traitor kinsman, Thou shouldst perceive my passion if these signs<br>Of prisonment were off me, and this hand<br>But owner of a [[Sword\|sword]]. By all oaths in one,<br>I and the justice of my [[Love\|love]] would make thee<br>A confessed traitor. O thou most perfidious<br>That ever gently looked, the void’st of [[Honour\|honour]]<br>That e’er bore gentle token, falsest cousin<br>That ever [[Blood\|blood]] made kin—call’st thou her thine?<br>I’ll prove it in my shackles, with these hands,<br>Void of appointment, that thou liest and art<br>A very thief in [[Love\|love]], a chaffy lord<br>Not worth the name of [[Villain\|villain]]. Had I as word<br>And these [[house]]-clogs away— |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Dear cousin [[Palamon]]— |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Cozener [[Arcite]], give me language such<br>As thou hast showed me feat. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not finding in The circuit of my breast any gross stuff<br>To form me like your blazon holds me to<br>This gentleness of answer—’tis your passion<br>That thus mistakes, the which, to you being enemy,<br>Cannot to me be kind. [[Honour]] and honesty<br>I cherish and depend on, howsoe’er<br>You skip them in me, and with them, fair coz,<br>I’ll maintain my proceedings. Pray be pleased<br>To show in generous terms your griefs, since that<br>Your question’s with your equal, who professes<br>To clear his own way with the mind and [[Sword\|sword]]<br>Of a true gentleman. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | That thou durst, [[Arcite]]! |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | My coz, my coz, you have been [[Well\|well]] advertised<br>How much I dare; you’ve seen me use my [[Sword\|sword]]<br>Against th’advice of [[Fear\|fear]]. Sure, of another<br>You would not hear me doubted, but your [[Silence\|silence]]<br>Should break out, though i’th’ sanctuary. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Sir, I have seen you move in such a place which [[Well\|well]]<br>Might justify your manhood; you were called<br>A good knight and a bold. But the whole week’s not<br>fair<br>If any day it rain: their valiant temper<br>Men lose when they incline to treachery,<br>And then they fight like compelled bears—would fly<br>Were they not tied. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Kinsman, you might as [[Well\|well]] Speak this and act it in your glass as to<br>His ear which now disdains you. |
| [[Palamon\|PALAMON]] | Come up to me, Quit me of these cold gyves, give me a [[Sword\|sword]],<br>Though it be rusty, and the [[Charity\|charity]]<br>Of one meal lend me. Come before me then,<br>A good [[Sword\|sword]] in thy hand, and do but say<br>That Emily is thine-I will forgive<br>The trespass thou hast done me, yea, my life,<br>If then thou carry’t; and brave souls in shades<br>That have died manly, which will seek of me<br>Some news from earth, they shall get none but this—<br>That thou art brave and noble. |
| [[Arcite\|ARCITE]] | Be content, Again betake you to your hawthorn [[house]].<br>With counsel of the [[Night\|night]] I will be here<br>With wholesome viands. These impediments<br>Will I file off. You shall have garments and<br>Perfumes to kill the smell o’th’ [[Prison\|prison]]. After,<br>When you shall stretch yourself and say but ’[[Arcite]],<br>I am in plight’, there shall be at your choice<br>Both [[Sword\|sword]] and armour. |
